天主教崇拜的一切神秘的丰盛与荣耀，都集中于弥撒祭。在这里，祭司职分的观念达到令人眩晕的顶峰；在这里，旁观者的虔敬与敬畏，也升至崇拜的最高境界。因为对虔诚的天主教徒而言，没有什么能比这样的崇拜举动更伟大、更庄严：上帝永恒的儿子，由祭司可见的手，为世人的罪，真实地在祭坛上被献给上帝。然而，尽管天主教的崇拜在这里远远超越了异教徒徒然的献祭和犹太教仅具预表性质的献祭，那种与犹太及希腊—罗马世界整个民众生活交织在一起的古老献祭礼仪，仍然对罗马天主教的圣餐礼仪施加了支配性的影响；尤其在整个罗马异教世界名义上归信之后，更是如此，以致这礼仪原初的单纯与纯洁几乎被遮蔽得面目全非。sacramentum（圣礼）完全被 sacrificium（献祭）掩盖，而 sacrificium 又被粗陋地物质化，并以牺牲十字架之祭为代价而受到高举。这种无穷无尽、被认为必不可少的重复，减损了基督的献祭。

弥撒祭的圣经根据十分薄弱，归根结底，不过是对《玛拉基书》一章10节及下节、《哥林多前书》十章21节、《希伯来书》五章6节、七章1节及下节、十三章10节等经文作过分拘泥于字面的解释，或干脆加以歪曲。《希伯来书》尤其常遭误用，尽管它极力教导的恰恰与此相反，即：基督教的崇拜废除了旧约的献祭制度；我们唯一的大祭司在父右边所献的祭，具有永远的效力，不可能再行重复（参较十章14节；七章23、24节）。

我们现在转入更具体的历史。尼西亚会议以前的教父一致将圣餐理解为教会的感恩祭；会众将祝圣后的饼酒元素，并在其中将自身，献给上帝。346这一观点本身完全无可指摘，但一旦把元素等同于基督的身体和血，并以物质主义的方式理解身体的临在，就很容易导向弥撒祭的教义。约在三世纪中叶，罗马教义的萌芽已见于居普良，与他高举教会权威的圣职祭司职分教义相联系。对他而言，sacerdotium（祭司职分）与 sacrificium（献祭）是相互关联的观念；对前者的犹太化理解，也助长了对后者同样的犹太化理解。祭司在圣餐中代替基督执行职务，347并在教会中施行真实的献祭。348然而，居普良并未明确说，基督就是这属灵祭的祭物；毋宁说，被献给上帝、并与基督联姻的，是基督奥秘的身体，即教会。349

弥撒祭的教义在尼西亚及后尼西亚时期的教父那里有了大得多的发展，尽管其中夹杂着许多含混不清之处与修辞上的夸张，又在象征的理解与粗陋的实在论理解之间多有摇摆；直到六世纪末，大格列高利 (Gregory the Great) 才使它在所有基本要点上定型。这些要点如下：

  1. 圣餐祭是教会最庄严的奥秘，使信徒充满神圣的敬畏。因此，人们常以 θυσία φοβερὰ*、φρικτὴ*、ἀναίμακτος*、sacrificium tremendum 等称谓，称之为可畏、令人战栗、无血的祭；东方的礼仪与讲道尤其如此。例如，《圣雅各礼仪》说：“主啊，我们向你献上这可畏而无血的祭。”因此，民众竟对如此庄严的举动漠不关心，金口约翰竟不得不哀叹，就更令人惊讶了：“每日献祭都是徒然，我们站在祭坛前也是徒然；竟无一人参与。”350

  2. 它不是在十字架之祭以外另加的新祭，而是那唯一献祭每日无血的重演和永久的施用。奥古斯丁一方面将它描述为 sacramentum memoriae（纪念的圣礼），即对基督献祭之死的象征性纪念；对此当然无可异议。351但另一方面，他又称举行圣餐是基督身体的 verissimum sacrificium（最真实的献祭）。他说，从使徒的日子起，藉着主教们确实无误的承继，直到我们这个时代，教会一直在基督的身体中向上帝献上（immolat）感恩的祭。但教会同时也作为基督的身体，与基督一同将自己献给上帝。既然众人同属一个身体，众人也就一同成为同一个祭。352按照金口约翰的说法，各处所献的，都是同一位基督，也是完整的基督。这并非与大祭司从前所献的不同的祭，而是我们始终献上同一个祭，或毋宁说，我们是在纪念这一献祭。353如果金口约翰在别处未曾使用如此强烈的说法：“当你看见主被杀，躺在那里，而祭司站在祭前”，或“基督被杀，躺在祭坛上”，那么最后这句话就显然有利于象征性的理解。354

  3. 这祭是摩西之祭所预表的实体，两者的关系犹如实体与预表性的影儿的关系。麦基洗德 (Melchizedek) 以饼酒所献的无血之祭，尤其预示了这祭。因此，希拉里 (Hilary)、哲罗姆、奥古斯丁、金口约翰 (Chrysostom) 及其他教父，根据《希伯来书》第七章那段众所周知的对比，都极其重视麦基洗德的献祭。

  4. 这祭所献的是耶稣基督的身体；这身体真实地临在于教会的祭坛上，正如它从前在十字架的祭坛上一样，如今又藉着他的祭司将自己献给上帝。因此，礼仪中常有这样的措辞：“基督，我们的上帝啊，你既是献祭者，也是被献者。”然而，正如我们已经说过的，奥古斯丁将整个蒙救赎的教会向上帝献上自身这一真实而重要的道德观念，与此联系起来。礼仪中的祷文也是如此。355

  5. 献上这祭是基督教祭司独有的特权。后来的罗马天主教神学家将“你们应当如此行（ποιεῖτε*），为的是记念我”这句话，理解为“你们应当如此献祭”，并把这一命令限于使徒及其职分上的继承者；然而，这显然是劝勉所有信徒纪念基督赎罪的死，所指的是 communio sacramenti（圣礼的共享），而非 immolatio sacrificii（祭物的奉献）。

  6. 这祭对教会全体，包括其中已经离世的肢体，都有功效，使他们获得礼拜祷文中所祈求的恩赐。

所有古代礼仪都以圣徒相通从未中断这一确信为基础，并包含对已故先辈与弟兄的纪念和代祷。他们被认为不是在炼狱中，而是在与上帝相交、圣洁与福乐不断增进的状态中，怀着虔诚的渴望，等候万事成全的大日。

这些祈求福乐增进的祷告，后来显得十分不合宜，却构成了一个过渡：从起初单纯纪念已故圣徒，包括族长、先知和使徒，转向为炼狱中受苦的灵魂代求；罗马教会自六世纪以来便采用这种代祷。356希腊与俄罗斯教会至今仍使用的《金口约翰礼仪》，其纪念亡者的祷文如下：“我们又为那些在信仰中离世的人，向你献上这理所当然的事奉，就是我们的祖先、先辈、族长、先知、使徒、传道者、传福音者、殉道者、认信者、童贞者，以及一切在信仰中得以完全的义人之灵……尤其是至圣、无玷污、极其可赞、荣耀的圣母、上帝之母、永为童贞的马利亚……圣先知、先驱与施洗者约翰，圣洁、荣耀、备受颂赞的众使徒，以及你所有的圣徒；上帝啊，求你因他们的祈祷垂顾我们。又求你记念一切怀着复活得永生的盼望而离世的人，使他们在你面光照耀之处得享安息。”

耶路撒冷的区利罗 (Cyril of Jerusalem) 在其第五篇、也是最后一篇奥秘教理讲授中，专论圣餐祭与敬拜上帝的礼仪，并对圣餐中为亡者所作的代祷作了如下描述：“当属灵的祭、无血的敬拜礼举行之时，我们在这赎罪祭前祈求上帝，使全教会得享平安，使世界蒙福；又为皇帝、士兵和囚犯，为患病和受苦的人，为一切贫穷困乏的人祷告。随后，我们也纪念那些已经睡了的人，就是族长、先知、使徒、殉道者，使上帝因他们的祷告和代求而悦纳我们的祈祷；总之，我们为一切离开我们的人祷告，因为我们相信，当这令人肃然敬畏的圣祭陈列在我们面前时，为那些灵魂所献的祷告，对他们有极大的帮助。”357

这显然已接近拉丁教会后来关于炼狱的观念。甚至圣奥古斯丁也与德尔图良一样，明确将以下教导视为古老的传统：活人所献的圣餐祭、代祷或 suffragia，以及施舍，都有益于已经离世的信徒，使主对待他们的怜悯，超过他们按其罪所当受的。358他那高尚的母亲莫尼卡 (Monica) 临终时告诉他，可以随意安葬她的身体，不必为此挂心；她只求他在主的祭坛前记念她的灵魂。359

与此相连的，是人在死亡与复活之间的中间状态中悔改并得洁净的观念。奥古斯丁同样从《马太福音》十二章32节和《哥林多前书》三章15节引申出这一观念，但主要仍将它视为一种意见。360根据这些及类似的经文，并在先前犹太教和异教观念与习俗的影响下，大格列高利之后产生了罗马天主教的炼狱之火教义：尚未完全的信徒，在适于进入天国以前，仍须洁净其罪的渣滓；同时也产生了专为亡者举行弥撒的制度。在这一制度中，对基督那一次献上、永远有效之祭的感恩纪念，其歪曲达到了顶点，而共同领受圣餐的观念则完全消失了。361

总的说来，在举行主餐时，圣礼不断退居于献祭之后。在各国的罗马天主教堂中，人们都可以看见并听见在正祭坛举行的隆重弥撒；在那里，信徒会众并不参与领受圣餐，而仅仅是祭司献祭行为的旁观者。领圣餐往往于清晨在侧祭坛匆匆举行。

§ 97. 圣餐的举行

参看下一节所引的礼仪学文献，尤其是丹尼尔 (Daniel)、尼尔 (Neale) 和弗里曼 (Freeman) 的著作。

圣餐祭的举行与圣餐的领受，是主日公共敬拜的中心和顶峰；敬拜的其余各部分都作为其预备与陪衬。古代礼仪在本质上、也几乎全部都是圣餐的祷文与仪式；除此以外，仅有一些洗礼程式和为慕道者所作的祷告。礼仪一词（λειτουργία*）本来涵盖敬拜上帝的一切部分，在狭义上则指圣餐或弥撒的举行。

天主教与福音派的敬拜在此有一个根本区别：前者以弥撒祭为中心，后者则以讲道为中心。

古代大公教会的礼仪，尽管在细节上，尤其在开头的部分，各有不同，但在基本要点上却是一致的，特别是在饼酒元素祝圣前后的祷文上。它们都重述福音书中庄严的设立圣餐之言362（某些景教与一性派礼仪程式的叙利亚文抄本除外），不仅将这些话理解为宣告，也理解为具有成事的效力；它们都包含祝圣、代祷和领圣餐的仪式；它们都祈求圣灵降临于饼酒元素之上，使其成圣，并使其真正成为基督身体和血的载体（罗马礼仪除外）；它们都首先将圣餐理解为献祭，然后在献祭的基础上，将其理解为共同领受圣餐。

狭义的圣餐仪式称为 Anaphora（奉献礼），或 canon missae（弥撒正典），在慕道者礼拜结束之后开始（慕道者礼拜主要由读经和讲道组成，一直延续至奉献礼，即预备饼酒并将其置于祭坛上）。它以祭司所说的 Ἄνω τὰς καρδίας*，或 Sursum corda（“当举心向上”）开始：这是劝勉信徒虔诚地举起心来，参与祷告；会众回答：Habemus ad Dominum，“我们举心归向主。”随后是劝勉：“我们当感谢主。”会众应答：“这是合宜而正当的。”363

奉献礼的第一个主要仪式，是效法救主设立圣餐时的榜样，献上大感恩祷文，即 εὐλογία* 或 εὐχαριστία*。在这祷告中，祭司为创造和救赎的一切恩赐感谢上帝；诗班通常以所谓三圣颂或撒拉弗颂（《以赛亚书》六章3节），以及凯旋的和散那颂（《马太福音》二十章9节），结束感恩：“圣哉，圣哉，圣哉，万军之主；天地充满你的荣耀。高高在上和散那！奉主名来的，是应当称颂的！高高在上和散那！”

随后是饼酒元素的祝圣与奉献，藉着纪念基督生平的重大事实，重述福音书或保罗所记的设立圣餐之言，并祈求圣灵降临而举行；圣灵使饼酒奥秘地转变为圣礼中基督的身体和血。364这种祈求圣灵降临的祷告365见于东方所有礼仪，拉丁教会却没有，因为后者将祝圣完全归因于基督设立圣餐之言的效力。各礼仪中设立圣餐之言的措辞并不相同。366拉丁教会还引入了高举已祝圣之饼酒的仪式，不过这直到十一世纪贝伦加尔 (Berengar) 论争之后才出现，目的是让民众有机会藉着敬拜圣体饼，表明他们相信基督真实临在于圣礼之中。

再举一例：在最古老、最重要的礼仪之一《圣雅各礼仪》中，祝圣与奉献的祷文如下。说完设立圣餐之言后，祭司继续说：

“祭司：我们这些罪人，记念他赐生命的受难、施行拯救的十字架、他的死和第三日从死里复活，他升上高天，坐在你——他的上帝与父——的右边，以及他荣耀而可畏的第二次显现；那时他将在荣耀中降临，审判活人死人，照各人的行为报应各人；主啊，我们向你献上这可畏而无血的祭；367恳求你不要按我们的罪待我们，也不要照我们的罪孽报应我们，却要按你的良善和对人不可言喻的慈爱，涂抹那攻击我们这些向你恳求之人的字据，将你属天而永恒的恩赐赏给我们；上帝啊，你为爱你之人所预备的，是眼睛未曾看见、耳朵未曾听见、人心也未曾想到的。慈爱的主啊，不要因我的缘故，因我的罪，弃绝你的百姓。

他重复三遍：因为你的百姓和你的教会向你祈祷。

会众：主上帝，全能的父啊，求你怜悯我们！

祭司：全能的上帝啊，求你怜悯我们！

上帝，我们的救赎主啊，求你怜悯我们！

上帝啊，求你照你丰盛的怜悯怜恤我们，将你至圣的灵降在我们和这里所陈设的礼物之上；他是主，是赐生命者，与你这上帝与父，并你的独生子，同坐一个宝座，同掌王权，同一本质，同为永恒；368他曾在律法、先知和你的新约中说话，曾在约旦河以鸽子的形状降在我们的主耶稣基督身上，住在他身上，又曾于五旬节在你圣洁荣耀的锡安楼房里，以火焰舌头的形状降在你的圣使徒身上。主啊，求你将这同一位圣灵降在我们和这里所陈设的圣礼物之上，使他以其圣洁、良善、荣耀的临在，将这饼分别为圣，使之成为你基督的圣体。369

会众：阿们。

祭司：并使这杯成为你基督的宝血。

会众：阿们。

祭司（低声）：使领受这些圣物的人得蒙赦罪，得享永生，灵魂身体得以成圣，结出善行，并使你建立在信心磐石上的圣大公教会得以坚固，阴间的权柄不能胜过她；救她脱离一切谬误、一切绊脚之事和不敬虔的人，保守她，直到万事的终结。”

祝圣礼之后是代祷，有时十分冗长，为教会、各阶层的人、生者和死者祈求；所纪念的死者，从义人亚伯直到马利亚、使徒、殉道者以及乐园中的圣徒；最后是主祷文。对于各项代祷和主祷文，会众或诗班都回应阿们。至此，圣餐献祭礼告终。

接着是领圣餐，即领受已经祝圣的饼酒。开始时宣告：“圣物归圣者”，370继以Kyrie eleison（求主怜悯），或（如《革利免礼仪》所用的）Gloria in Excelsis（荣耀归于至高上帝颂）：“在至高之处荣耀归与上帝，在地上平安、善意归与人。371和散那归于大卫的子孙！奉主名来的，是应当称颂的；上帝是主，他已显现于我们中间。”主教和教士先领，然后会众领。《革利免礼仪》中的分发用语很简单：“基督的身体”；“基督的血，生命之杯”，372领受者回答：“阿们。”其他礼仪中的用语则较长。373

这一神圣礼仪以感恩祷告、诗篇和祝福结束。

圣餐每日举行，或至少每逢主日举行。教会劝勉信徒经常领圣餐，尤其是在重大节期。在北非，有人每日领受，有人每逢主日领受，也有人领受得更少。374奥古斯丁 (Augustine) 认为，这应由每位信徒按自己的需要决定，但他在一处说：“圣餐是我们日用的饮食。”每日领圣餐的习惯，与当时流行的、对主祷文第四项祈求的奥秘解释有关。巴西尔 (Basil) 每周领圣餐四次。马西利亚的根纳迪乌斯 (Gennadius of Massilia) 劝人至少每周领受一次。在东方，第四世纪以后，似乎惯例是每年只领受一次，或仅在重大场合领受。金口约翰 (Chrysostom) 常常责备那些冷淡的人，他们到教会只为听道，或虽参加圣餐献祭礼，却不领圣餐。我们前面已经引过他论及这种疏忽的一段话。后来有些会议威胁说，凡平信徒若不至少在圣诞节、复活节和五旬节领圣餐，就要被革除教籍。

东方和北非教会盛行婴儿领圣餐这一不相宜的习俗；它似乎是由婴儿洗礼推衍而来，奥古斯丁和英诺森一世 (Innocent I.) 都以《约翰福音》六章53节为依据加以提倡。在希腊教会中，这一习俗延续至今；但在拉丁教会中，第九世纪以后，它便受到争议或禁止，因为使徒（林前十一28、29）要求领受者省察自己，以此为合宜领受的条件。375

伴随这一习俗，出现了最早的、也只是例外的单形领圣餐（communio sub una specie）事例；此前，居普良 (Cyprian) 时代迦太基 (Carthage) 的一个小女孩曾因领受圣餐酒而醉倒。但不让平信徒领杯的做法，直到第十二、十三世纪才出现；它违背了主明确的命令：“你们都喝这个”，并且与一种迷信的恐惧有关，惟恐酒洒出来就亵渎了主的血，也与祭司权力的发展有关；后来，经院哲学的伴随说（doctrine of concomitance）又为它作了辩护。

希腊教会的惯例是把饼蘸在酒里，用匙将饼酒一同送给领受者。

为病人和囚犯举行家庭圣餐及事后补领圣餐，将未祝圣的余饼分给未领圣餐的人，以及在复活节将祝圣的饼酒或其代用品376送给远方的主教或教会，作为相交的凭据，这些习俗都十分古老。

希腊教会使用有酵饼，拉丁教会使用无酵饼。这一区别最终引起了错综复杂的争论。

在酒中掺水被视为不可缺少的，并得到种种奥秘性的解释；最主要的是联系到耶稣在十字架上肋旁流出的血和水。

§ 98. 各种礼仪：起源与内容

J. 戈阿尔 (J. Goar，一位博学的多明我会士，† 1653)：《希腊礼仪书》(Εὐχολόγιον*, sive Rituale Graecorum, 等)。希腊文与拉丁文。巴黎 (Par.)，1647年（另一版于1740年在威尼斯出版）。约斯·阿洛伊斯·阿塞马尼 (Jos. Aloys. Assemani，罗马天主教)：《普世教会礼仪法典……其中收录东西方教会的礼典、弥撒书、主教礼典、日课、双联名册等》(Codex Liturgicus ecclesiae universae, ... in quo continentur libri rituales, missales, pontificales, officia, dypticha, etc., ecclesiarum Occidentis et Orientis)（在教皇卜尼法斯十四世 (Boniface XIV.) 赞助下出版）。罗马 (Rom.)，1749—1766年，13卷。欧塞布·勒诺多 (Euseb. Renaudot，罗马天主教)：《东方礼仪集》(Liturgiarum orientalium collectio)。巴黎，1716年（1847年重印），2卷。L. A. 穆拉托里 (L. A. Muratori，罗马天主教，† 1750)：《古罗马礼仪》(Liturgia Romana vetus)。威尼斯 (Venet.)，1748年，2卷（收录利奥 (Leo)、格拉修 (Gelasius) 和贵格利一世 (Gregory I.) 的三部罗马圣礼书，以及《哥特弥撒书》(Missale Gothicum)，并附有博学的导论《论礼仪事宜》(De rebus liturgicis)）。W. 帕尔默 (W. Palmer，英国圣公会)：《礼仪起源》(Origines Liturgicae)。伦敦 (Lond.)，1832年（另有1845年版），2卷（特别论及英国圣公会礼仪）。Ths. 布雷特 (Ths. Brett)：《基督教会举行圣餐所用主要礼仪汇集，尤重古代礼仪（译成英文），并附论述》(A Collection of the Principal Liturgies used in the Christian Church in the celebration of the Eucharist, particularly the ancient, with a Dissertation upon them)。伦敦，1838年（465页）。W. 特罗洛普 (W. Trollope，英国圣公会)：《圣雅各的希腊礼仪》(The Greek Liturgy of St. James)。爱丁堡 (Edinb.)，1848年。H. A. 丹尼尔 (H. A. Daniel，路德宗，德国最博学的礼仪学家)：《普世教会礼仪法典撮要》(Codex Liturgicus ecclesiae universae in epitomem redactus)。莱比锡 (Lips.)，1847年及后，4卷（第i卷收罗马礼仪，第iv卷收东方礼仪）。Fr. J. 莫内 (Fr. J. Mone，罗马天主教)：《第二至第六世纪的拉丁与希腊弥撒》(Lateinische u. Griechische Messen aus dem 2ten his 6ten Jahrhundert)。美因河畔法兰克福 (Frankf. a. M.)，1850年（附有论高卢、非洲及罗马弥撒的宝贵论文）。J. M. 尼尔 (J. M. Neale，† 1866，英国圣公会最博学的礼典与礼仪学家，三十年来每日研究东方礼仪，几乎能熟记于心)：《礼仪四部集；即圣金口约翰、圣雅各、圣马可的神圣弥撒，并附莫萨拉布礼序》(Tetralogia liturgica; sive S. Chrysostom, S. Jacobi, S. Marci divinae missae: quibus accedit ordo Mozarabicus)。伦敦，1849年。同一作者：《圣马可、圣雅各、圣革利免、圣金口约翰、圣巴西尔的礼仪，或亚历山大、耶路撒冷、君士坦丁堡诸教会所用礼仪》(The Liturgies of S. Mark, S. James, S. Clement, S. Chrysostom, S. Basil, or according to the use of the churches of Alexandria, Jerusalem, Constantinople)。伦敦，1859年及下年（希腊文原本；这些礼仪的英译本，附导论及附录，也于1859年在伦敦出版）。另参尼尔的《神圣东方教会史》(History of the Holy Eastern Church)，伦敦，1850年；总论，第2卷；以及他的《礼仪学与教会史论文集》(Essays on Liturgiology and Church History)，伦敦，1863年。（后者献给莫斯科都主教菲拉列特 (Philaret)，汇集了作者发表于《基督徒纪念者》(Christian Remembrancer) 的多篇博学论文，论述罗马与高卢的日课书、教会的祝文、莫萨拉布与安布罗修礼仪、礼仪引文等。）前面已引过的英国圣公会神学家弗里曼 (Freeman) 那部精神相近的著作，同样多论古代礼仪，偏重西方礼仪；尼尔则对东方礼典怀有特别的敬意。（另参本生 (Bunsen)：《基督教与人类》(Christianity and Mankind)，伦敦，1854年，第vii卷，其中收有《礼仪遗存》(Reliquiae Liturgicae)；欧文派 (Irvingite) 著作：《教会礼仪及其他神圣职事读本》(Readings upon the Liturgy and other Divine Offices of the Church)，伦敦，1848—1854年；赫夫林 (Höfling)：《礼仪文献集》(Liturgisches Urkundenbuch)，莱比锡 (Leipz.)，1854年。）

礼仪（Liturgy）377在教会用语中，378泛指公共崇拜的秩序与施行，特指圣餐的举行；继而又指举行圣餐时所用的祷告书或祷文集。拉丁教会称公开举行的圣餐礼为弥撒（Mass），称礼仪书为sacramentarium（圣礼书）、rituale（礼典）、missale（弥撒书），也称libri mysteriorum（奥秘之书），或简称libelli（小册子）。

犹太人的崇拜，动作多于言词，但也包括固定的祷告和诗篇（如诗一一三至一一八），以及会众的阿们（参林前十四16）。异教的希腊人和罗马人在献祭时，也有一些固定的祷告和祝圣用语；不过这些并未写成文字，而是靠口传保存。相反，印度文献中却有礼仪书，甚至《古兰经》也载有规定的祷告格式。

新约既没有给我们一部礼仪书，也没有给我们一部礼典，却提供了二者的主要要素。主祷文，以及设立洗礼和圣餐时所说的话，乃是活的胚芽；教会最美好的祷告、洗礼用语和圣餐用语，无论口传还是书面，都是由此生长出来的。在西方教会，彼得的认信和洗礼的信仰宣认逐渐发展成为《使徒信经》；它除了具有教义意义以外，也有礼仪上的功用，作为慕道受洗者与信徒公开的信仰宣认。东方教会则用《尼西亚信经》代替。天军的颂歌是《荣耀归于至高上帝颂》(Gloria in Excelsis) 的基础。《启示录》本身就是一幅崇高的礼仪异象。与这些同列的还有《诗篇》；基督教会作为合法的继承者承受了它，它也历来为公众的造就提供了最丰富的材料。

在尼西亚会议以前的时期，我们尚未发现礼仪书的踪迹。当然，各教会都逐渐形成了固定的崇拜秩序；在使徒所建立的会众中，这种秩序多少可以追溯到使徒的渊源，但随着时间推移，又有所增补和改变，直到第四世纪以前，都仅靠口传保存。因为圣礼的举行，尤其是圣餐，属于守秘规制（Disciplina arcani），被视为教会至圣之事，为免受亵渎，不让犹太人、异教徒，甚至慕道者观看；这是由于误解了主不可把珍珠丢在猪前的警告，又仿效撒摩特拉基 (Samothracian) 和厄琉西斯 (Eleusinian) 的秘密仪式。379随着罗马帝国中异教的衰亡，守秘规制（Disciplina arcani）逐渐消失，圣礼的施行也成为向所有人开放的公开礼仪。

因此，从第四、第五世纪起，我们便发现大量成文的礼仪书，不仅见于正统的大公教会，也见于分裂派（如聂斯托利派和基督一性派）。这些礼仪书大多冠以使徒之名，但就其现存形式而言，它们与所谓《使徒宪章》和《使徒教规》一样，不能出自使徒；其使徒渊源甚至远不及《使徒信经》。我们从新约、护教士和尼西亚会议以前教父的著作所能推知的原始基督教崇拜，是十分简朴的，而这些礼仪与之反差过大。它们也包含一些神学术语，如ὁμοούσιος*（论及上帝的儿子）和θεοτόκος*（论及童贞女马利亚）；其中有些还包含整篇《尼西亚信经》，连同381年第二次大公会议所作的增补，以及对殉道者和圣徒崇敬、修道主义的暗示，这些都明确指向尼西亚及后尼西亚时期。然而，它们都以共同的礼仪传统为基础；这一传统的基本要素可上溯至更早的时代，其中某些方面也许可以追溯到使徒时期，甚至是犹太教崇拜经由犹太基督徒会众传承下来的。否则，就无法圆满解释它们之间的亲缘关系；这种关系在许多方面令人想起符类福音之间的关系。这些古代大公教会礼仪，在祷文的措辞、数量、长短和次序，以及其他非本质之处彼此有别，却在圣餐礼最重要的部分彼此一致。它们的差异太大，不能说都出自同一原本；但又太过相似，不能说各自完全独立产生。380

所有古代礼仪都将动作与祷告结合起来，并按照犹太人的习惯，以会众的参与为前提；会众经常应答祭司的祷告，藉此表明他们自身的祭司身份。这些应答有时只是简单的阿们，有时是Kyrie eleison（求主怜悯），有时则是与祭司的某种对答：

祭司：愿主与你们同在！

会众：也与你的灵同在！

祭司：你们当举起心来！

会众：我们举心向主。

祭司：我们应当感恩！

会众：这是合宜而正当的。

礼仪中的某些部分，如信经、撒拉弗颂（Seraphic Hymn）、主祷文，由祭司和会众共同诵念或歌唱。起初原是要全体信徒会众381一同应答；但随着教阶原则的发展，民主和民众参与的因素逐渐消退，执事或诗班取代会众作出应答，在礼仪语言不为民众所理解的地方尤其如此。382

几种最古老的礼仪，如圣革利免（St. Clement）和圣雅各（St. James）的礼仪，早已不再使用，仅具有历史价值。另一些，如圣巴西尔（St. Basil）、圣金口约翰（St. Chrysostom）的礼仪，以及罗马礼仪，历经不同时代的种种修改和增补，至今仍在希腊和拉丁教会中使用。其中许多最有价值的部分，经由拉丁弥撒书，传入了圣公会、路德宗以及一些改革宗教会的礼仪书和礼典。

然而，总体而言，它们所流露的气息，与新教礼仪全然不同，连圣公会的礼仪也不例外。因为在这些古代礼仪中，圣餐祭都是中心，一切祷告和礼仪都围绕它展开。这种为活人和死人献祭的举动，本身就是完整的崇拜；讲道完全不是必需的，事实上通常也予以省略。相反，在新教中，主餐几乎纯粹是领餐（Communion），而讲道则是每次通常崇拜的主要内容。

东方与西方礼仪之间，有以下几个显著的区别：

  1. 东方礼仪保留了尼西亚会议以前将公共崇拜分为两部分的做法：λειτουργία κατηχουμένων*，即Missa Catechumenorum（慕道者弥撒），主要以教导为主；以及λειτουργία τῶν πιστῶν*，即Missa Fidelium（信徒弥撒），其中包含正式的圣餐礼。随着政教联合和婴儿洗礼的普遍施行，这一区分失去了原有的意义。拉丁礼仪则将两部分连成一个整体。

  2. 东方礼仪在设立圣餐的话之后，包含明确的求圣灵降临祷文；若无此祷告，饼酒便未完全成圣。高卢（Gallican）礼仪中也有这种做法的痕迹。但罗马礼仪完全没有这种祈求，并认为祭司复述设立圣餐的话，就使饼酒成圣。这始终是希腊教会与罗马教会之间的争论点。大贵格利（Gregory the Great）断言，使徒祝圣时所用的，只有设立圣餐的话和主祷文。383然而，新约对此并未提供任何资料，他身处第六世纪，又从何得知呢？经上没有一处提到为饼酒所作的invocatio Spiritus Sancti（求圣灵降临的祷告）；只提到主在设立圣餐的话之前祝谢，而这也可能构成一种祝圣的行动，虽然既不是希腊教会、也不是罗马教会所理解的那种意义。再者，设立圣餐的话：“这是我的身体”等，并不是向上帝说的，而是向门徒说的；可以说，它们表明了主祝福的结果。384 3. 东方礼仪与新教教会较为相似，容许使用各种本地语言，如希腊语、叙利亚语、亚美尼亚语、科普特语等；罗马弥撒却因追求划一，而牺牲一切本地语言，独用拉丁语，以致民众无法理解。

  3. 东方礼仪可以说是救赎历史的一出象征性戏剧，每个主日都几乎原样重演。此前的晚祷表现创造、堕落和对基督的热切盼望；主日早晨的主要崇拜则展现基督从降生到升天的一生；祷告和读经都伴随着祭司与执事相应的象征性动作：点燃和熄灭蜡烛、开门关门、亲吻祭坛和福音书、在额头、口和胸前画十字、摇动香炉、频繁更换礼服、列队行进、屈膝和俯伏。整个希腊和俄罗斯正教的崇拜都具有十分鲜明的东方特色，其象征性仪式的辉煌与排场，超过了罗马教会。385

罗马弥撒也是对救赎历史，尤其是基督受难和赎罪之死的戏剧性纪念与再现，但更具教导性质；从Confiteor（认罪文）到Postcommunio（领餐后祷文），它所阐明的，与其说是客观的历史，不如说是救赎在主观上的应用。它给予象征性动作的余地较少，给予言词与歌唱的余地较多，并且更紧密地遵循教会年历的进程，为各大节期采用不同的祝文和序祷文，386由此获得多样性。在这一点上，它更接近新教崇拜；然而，后者完全抛弃象征性的帷幕，而以讲道为中心。

每一种东方礼仪都有两个主要部分。第一部分包括献祭礼（Anaphora或Oblation；canon Missae，即弥撒正典）以前的祷告和动作，直到Sursum corda（举心向主）；第二部分则从献祭礼开始，直到结束。

第一部分又分为慕道者弥撒和信徒弥撒，后者直到Sursum corda（举心向主）为止。这一部分包括开端祷文、进堂咏（Introit、Ingressa或Antiphon）、小进堂礼（Little Entrance）、三圣颂（Trisagion）、经课、福音后的祷告，以及遣散慕道者；随后是信徒的祷告、大进堂礼（Great Entrance）、奉献礼、平安之吻和信经。

献祭礼包括圣餐大感恩祷文、纪念耶稣生平、设立圣餐的话、奉献饼酒、求圣灵降临的祷告、为活人和死人的大代祷、主祷文，最后是领餐及其特有的祷告和动作、感恩，以及遣散礼。387

§ 99. 东方礼仪

若将修订本、改编本和译本计算在内，古代礼仪总数大概超过一百种。但按近代研究，它们都可归入五六个系统，并可按其发源和使用的教会命名：耶路撒冷（或安提阿）、亚历山大、君士坦丁堡、以弗所和罗马。388其中大多数属于东方教会；因为总体而言，东方教会比西方教会创造得多，也容许更大的多样性，而西方教会则在组织和崇拜上追求划一。在东方礼仪中，希腊礼仪最为古老，也最为重要。

  1. 圣革利免礼仪。这一礼仪载于《使徒宪章》（Apostolic Constitutions）第八卷，与该书一同被错误地归于罗马主教革利免名下。389这是现存最古老的完整崇拜程序，很可能于第四世纪初编成于东方。390它与圣雅各及耶路撒冷的区利罗（Cyril of Jerusalem）的礼仪最为相近，因此可以视为耶路撒冷系统的一个分支。我们不知道哪些教会使用过它，甚至不知道它是否实际使用过。它是一种规范性的礼仪，其主要价值在于显示尼西亚或尼西亚以前的崇拜形式，与后来的增补和改动之间的差别。

革利免礼仪严格区分慕道者的崇拜与信徒的崇拜。391它包含分发圣餐饼酒时最简单的用语：“基督的身体”和“基督的血，生命之杯”，会众对每一句都应以“阿们”。在纪念亡者时，它没有提及任何圣徒的具体名字，甚至没有提及上帝之母；她直到431年以弗所会议以后，才在公共崇拜中占有一席之地。这一礼仪也省略了祭司的若干开端祷文。最后，它没有《尼西亚信经》，甚至没有主祷文；而主祷文是附于其他一切圣餐祷文之后的，并且按照某些教会法学家的原则，是绝对不可缺少的。392

  1. 圣雅各礼仪。传统将这一礼仪归于主的兄弟、耶路撒冷主教雅各。393当然，它不可能出自他手；仅就其中出现的《尼西亚信经》以及ὁμοούσιος*和θεοτόκος*这两个属于尼西亚及后尼西亚神学的用语而言，就足以说明这一点。以下这段话也表明其成书年代晚得多：“让我们纪念至圣、无玷、极其荣耀、蒙福的上帝之母、永贞女马利亚，以及众圣徒，使我们藉着他们的祷告和代求，得蒙怜悯。”关于使用这一礼仪的首次明确记载，见于第五世纪中叶左右的君士坦丁堡的普罗克鲁斯（Proclus of Constantinople）。然而，就实质而言，它无论如何都是最古老的礼仪之一，并且早在第四世纪就必已使用；因为卒于386年的耶路撒冷的区利罗，在其《奥秘教理讲授》（Mystagogic Catechesis）第五讲中所引用的礼仪，与它逐字相合。这一礼仪是为耶路撒冷教会编定的；在为普世教会祷告的开头，称这教会为“荣耀的锡安，众教会之母”。394

就内容和措辞而言，它是古代礼仪中最重要的一种，也是许多礼仪的丰饶母体，其中圣巴西尔 (St. Basil) 和圣金口约翰 (St. Chrysostom) 的礼仪须另行述及。395它传遍整个安提阿牧首辖区，甚至远及塞浦路斯 (Cyprus)、西西里 (Sicily) 和卡拉布里亚 (Calabria)；但在伊斯兰教徒征服之后，东方正统教会便以拜占庭礼仪取代了它。如今，耶路撒冷和希腊的一些岛屿仍使用这一礼仪，但每年仅在10月23日圣雅各节举行一次。396

叙利亚文的雅各礼仪是从希腊文自由译出的；其中求圣灵降临的祷文较为详尽，其他祷文则较为简短，显出它出于较晚的年代。它是三十九种基督一性派 (Monophysite) 礼仪的源头，这些礼仪至今仍在分裂出去的叙利亚教徒，即雅各派 (Jacobites) 中使用。397

  1. 圣马可礼仪，或称亚历山大礼仪。这一礼仪被归于那位著名的福音书作者；按照传统，他也是埃及首都教会及教理学校的创立者。当然，这种起源说包含了惊人的时代错误，因为该礼仪载有381年的《尼西亚—君士坦丁堡信经》。它目前的形式很可能出自亚历山大主教区利罗 (Cyril，卒于444年)；正统派和基督一性派都声称，他支持各自关于基督位格的教义。398无论如何，它与冠以区利罗之名的礼仪完全一致。

它与其他礼仪不同之处，在于为生者与死者所作的大代祷，置于设立圣餐之辞和求圣灵降临的祷文之前，而非之后。它最初以希腊文写成，后来译为科普特文和阿拉伯文。埃及一直使用它到第十二世纪，此后便由拜占庭礼仪取代。科普特人仍保留着它。埃塞俄比亚圣餐正典祷文是它的一个分支。同属这一系统的，有三种科普特礼仪和十种埃塞俄比亚礼仪。399

  1. 埃德萨 (Edessa) 或美索不达米亚 (Mesopotamia) 礼仪，又称众使徒礼仪。这一礼仪的起源被追溯至使徒达太 (Thaddaeus，又作Adaeus) 和马里斯 (Maris)，仅限于聂斯托利派 (Nestorians) 使用。后来从它衍生出以下聂斯托利派礼仪：(1) 解经家狄奥多尔 (Theodore the Interpreter) 礼仪；(2) 聂斯托利 (Nestorius) 礼仪；(3) 麻风病人纳尔塞斯 (Narses the Leper) 礼仪；(4) 巴尔苏马斯 (Barsumas) 礼仪；(5) 马拉巴尔 (Malabar) 礼仪，或称圣多马礼仪。马拉巴尔多马基督徒的礼仪，已被迪安佩尔 (Diamper) 的修订者掺改了许多。400

  2. 圣巴西尔礼仪和圣金口约翰礼仪，合在一起构成拜占庭或君士坦丁堡礼仪，并且同时传入希腊—俄罗斯教会。二者均源于圣雅各礼仪，是该礼仪的简略形式。它们是活的礼仪书，不像革利免和雅各礼仪那样，已成为死的文献。

新凯撒利亚 (Neo-Caesarea) 主教巴西尔（卒于379年）的礼仪，在希腊和俄罗斯正教会中，于大斋期（棕枝主日除外）、显现节、复活节和圣诞节的前夕，以及圣巴西尔节（1月1日）诵用。亚美尼亚 (Armenian) 礼仪即由此而出。

圣金口约翰（卒于407年）的礼仪则用于其他所有主日。它是圣巴西尔礼仪的删节和改进本；自第六世纪起，借着君士坦丁堡几位杰出牧首的影响，它取代了圣雅各和圣马可的礼仪。由于现存抄本彼此差异甚大，其原始文本几乎无法确定。

现今希腊和俄罗斯的礼仪，其华丽甚至超过罗马礼仪，绝不可能在一切细节上都传自金口约翰的时代。诚然，正如普罗克鲁斯 (Proclus) 所说，人们认为金口约翰鉴于人性的软弱，曾在许多方面缩短君士坦丁堡的崇拜；但冠以他名字的礼仪，直到第七世纪仍称为“圣使徒礼仪”，看来不早于第八世纪才得了他的名字。

§ 100. 西方礼仪

西方教会的礼仪可分为三类：(1) 以弗所系统，其起源被追溯至约翰，包括莫扎拉布 (Mozarabic) 和高卢 (Gallican) 礼仪；(2) 罗马礼仪，当然，它与教皇制本身一样，也必定要说成传自圣彼得；(3) 安布罗斯 (Ambrosian) 和阿奎莱亚 (Aquileian) 礼仪，是前两者的混合。因此，这里的多样性不及东方。拉丁教会的趋向，在各地都强有力地推动划一，罗马礼仪最终排斥了其他一切礼仪。

  1. 古高卢礼仪。401它在许多特征上，如同高卢南部基督教的开端一样，指向亚细亚、以弗所的源头；就此而言，我们可以说它源于约翰。它在第五世纪形成了后来的形态。在它的编撰者，或更确切地说修订者之中，特别被提到的是普瓦捷的希拉里 (Hilary of Poictiers)。查理大帝 (Charlemagne) 时期，它被罗马礼仪取代。高卢主义在教会组织和治理上勇敢地主张自己的权利，在崇拜上却轻易地听任自己罗马化了。

古不列颠 (British) 礼仪无疑与高卢礼仪相同，但在盎格鲁—撒克逊人 (Anglo-Saxons) 归信之后，也同样被罗马礼仪取代。

  1. 古西班牙礼仪，或称哥特礼仪（虽然这一称呼并不正确），又名莫扎拉布礼仪。402这一礼仪在许多方面与高卢礼仪相近，很可能经由后者传来，同出于一个东方源头。它似乎在409年西哥特人 (West Goths) 入侵之前就已存在，因为其中没有亚流派 (Arian) 异端或君士坦丁堡礼仪制度影响的痕迹。403它目前的形式被归于塞维利亚的伊西多尔 (Isidore of Seville) 和633年的第四次托莱多会议 (Council of Toledo)。它在西班牙一直保持到第十三世纪，此后被罗马礼仪取代。404

它与高卢礼仪一样，除福音书和使徒书信外，也有选自旧约的诵读经课；405它的节期次序与罗马礼仪不同；在正式的献祭行动之前，还包含一段讲道式的劝勉。领圣餐前所用的宣告语 Sancta Sanctis（圣物归与圣者）、将圣饼掰为九份（纪念基督生平的九项奥秘）、每日领圣餐，以及由执事分杯，都使我们想起东方礼仪。莫扎拉布圣咏与格列高利圣咏十分相似，但也表现出某种独立的民族特性。406

  1. 我们对非洲礼仪的认识，仅来自德尔图良、居普良 (Cyprian) 和奥古斯丁著作中的零星引文；由这些引文，我们得知它属于罗马系统。

  2. 圣安布罗斯 (St. Ambrose) 礼仪。407它被归于这位著名的米兰主教（卒于397年），甚至被归于圣巴拿巴。可以确定的是，安布罗斯引入了诗篇和圣诗的应答式歌唱，并撰写了若干祷文、序祷和圣诗。据认为，他的继任者辛普利修斯 (Simplicius，公元397—400年) 对礼仪作了若干增补。许多成分源于哥特诸王统治时期（公元493—568年）和伦巴第诸王统治时期（公元568—739年）。

安布罗斯礼仪至今仍在米兰教区使用；在多次试图以罗马礼仪取代它而未果之后，亚历山大六世 (Alexander VI) 于1497年颁布特别诏书，以安布罗斯礼仪 (Ritus Ambrosianus) 之名予以确认。除了一些东方特色外，它在实质上与罗马礼仪一致，但既不具罗马礼仪那种意蕴深厚的简洁，也没有莫扎拉布礼仪的丰富充实。奉献祭物时的祷文与罗马礼仪不同；《使徒信经》直到奉献之后才诵读；教区内的一些圣徒被列入圣徒的正式名录；圣饼在主祷文 (Paternoster) 之前分发，并使用它自己特有的用语，等等。

长期用于阿奎莱亚 (Aquileia) 牧首辖区的礼仪，与安布罗斯礼仪相近，也同样介于罗马礼仪和具有东方性质的高卢礼仪之间。

  1. 按照传统，罗马礼仪的主要特征被归于使徒彼得，但从历史上只能追溯到第五世纪中叶。它无疑是逐渐发展为现今形式的。关于它的最古老文字记录，见于三部分别以利奥 (Leo)、格拉修斯 (Gelasius) 和格列高利 (Gregory) 三位教皇命名的圣礼书。

  1. 《利奥圣礼书》(Sacramentarium Leonianum) 被错误地归于教皇利奥一世（卒于461年），很可能出于第五世纪末，是一部编排没有章法的礼仪程式汇编。它于1735年首次根据维罗纳 (Verona) 的一部抄本刊行。408

  2. 《格拉修斯圣礼书》(Sacramentarium Gelasianum) 于1680年首次在罗马印行，被认为是罗马主教格拉修斯（† 492—496年）的著作；他确实编撰过一部《圣礼书》(Sacramentarium)。其中缺少许多自第七世纪以来便已施行的圣徒节日。

  3. 《格列高利圣礼书》(Sacramentarium Gregorianum)，由穆拉托里 (Muratori) 等人编辑出版。格列高利一世（590—604年）被誉为罗马《弥撒程序与正典》(Ordo et Canon Missae) 真正的创立者；这一礼仪经后世种种增补和修改，逐渐在拉丁教会取得几乎排他的通行地位，并获得特伦托会议 (Council of Trent) 的认可。

将罗马礼仪409各部分汇编成一册的书，称为《罗马弥撒经书》(Missale Romanum)；给祭司的指示则称为礼仪规程 (Rubricae)。410

§ 101. 礼仪服饰

除前引礼仪著作外，参看约翰·英格兰 (John England，已故美国南卡罗来纳州查尔斯顿罗马天主教主教，卒于1842年)：《弥撒圣祭所用服饰、仪式等的历史说明》(An Historical Explanation of the Vestments, Ceremonies, etc., appertaining to the holy Sacrifice of the Mass)，为《罗马弥撒经书》美国英语版的导论。费城 (Philad.)，1843年。Fr. 博克 (Fr. Bock，罗马天主教)：《中世纪礼仪服饰史》(Geschichte der liturgischen Gewänder des Mittelalters)。波恩 (Bonn)，1856年，2卷。C. Jos. 黑费勒 (C. Jos. Hefele)：《教会史、考古学与礼仪学论集》(Beiträge zur Kirchengeschichte, Archäologie und Liturgik)，第ii卷。图宾根 (Tüb.)，1864年，第150页及后。

公共崇拜庄严隆重的外在形式，以及圣职阶层与平信徒群体的严格区分，要求有相应的礼仪服饰，仿照犹太祭司制度与崇拜的先例，411用以象征圣职人员的等级和崇拜的不同部分。

希腊教会的礼仪服饰和饰物包括：执事所用的长衣 (sticharion)412和执事带 (orarion，或horarion)413；祭司所用的长衣 (sticharion)、祭披 (phelonion)414、腰带 (zone)415、颈带 (epitrachelion)416和袖套 (epimanikia)417；主教所用的主教袍 (saccos)418、肩带 (omophorion)419、膝饰 (epigonation)420和牧杖 (crozier)421。希腊人不使用主教冠 (mitre)。

拉丁教会的礼服包括：祭司所用的披肩 (amict，或humeral)422、白衣 (alb，即白色披衣或白罩衣)423、腰带 (cincture)424、臂带 (maniple)425、领带 (orarium，或stole)426；主教所用的祭披 (chasuble)427、宽袖长袍 (dalmatic)428、胸饰 (pectoral)429和主教冠 (mitre)430；大主教所用的肩带 (pallium)。此外还有主教戒指和权杖，即牧杖。

这些圣职服饰似乎几乎全都在第七世纪以前便已或多或少地使用，不过仅限于公共崇拜；每一种的确切年代已无法确定。祭司服饰的使用本身，事实上源于旧约，无疑是经由犹太基督教传入教会的，当然其间经过许多改变。君士坦丁大帝 (Constantine the Great) 曾赠给耶路撒冷主教马卡里乌斯 (Macarius) 一条华丽的金绣领带，供施洗时使用。

天主教礼仪学家自然赋予各种弥撒服饰以象征性的解释，其中一部分取自犹太祭司服饰不可否认的意义，431但另一部分却是任意的，因此也变化不定。例如，披肩表示使心思从散乱中收拢；白衣表示祭司的公义与圣洁；臂带表示善行的果实；领带表示祭司的职权；主教冠表示圣职人员的首领地位；戒指表示主教与教会的婚姻；牧杖则表示他对羊群的监督。

礼仪服饰的颜色有几个世纪一直是白色；正如犹太祭司的服饰以白色为主，白色是光明与救恩的象征。但后来逐渐确立了五种教会礼仪颜色。衣料各不相同，只是披肩和白衣规定须用细麻布（如旧约所定）。按照现今罗马教会的习惯，圣衣与其他圣器及圣水一样，必须由主教或专为此事委任的圣职人员祝圣。希腊人甚至在每次使用前都要祝圣。《罗马弥撒经书》(Roman Missal) 及其他礼仪书的礼规，对弥撒服饰的使用都有详细规定。

在最初五六个世纪，圣职人员日常生活中普遍穿着普通市民的服装；后来才逐渐仿效犹太祭司432和基督教修士，改穿适宜的职服，以表明他们高于平信徒的地位。迟至428年，罗马主教塞莱斯廷 (Celestine) 还曾责备一些高卢 (Gallic) 祭司，因为他们误解了《路加福音》十二章35节，将普遍使用的内衣 (tunica) 和外袍 (toga) 换成东方的修士服；他正确地提醒他们，圣职人员与其他人的区别，与其说应在外在服饰上，不如说应在教义与生活的纯洁上。433然而，后来的教皇和会议就这些外在事物制定了种种法规与罚则，特伦托会议 (council of Trent) 更规定了与祭司职分的尊严相称的职服。434

Original Text

CHAPTER VII. PUBLIC WORSHIP AND RELIGIOUS CUSTOMS AND CEREMONIES.

I. The ancient Liturgies; the Acts of Councils; and the ecclesiastical writers of the period.

  1. The archaeological and liturgical works of Martene, Mamachi, Bona, Muratori, Pelicia, Asseman, Renaudot, Binterim, and Staudenmeier, of the Roman Catholic church; and Bingham, Augusti, Siegel, Alt, Piper, Neale, and Daniel, of the Protestant.

§ 74. The Revolution in Cultus.

The change in the legal and social position of Christianity with reference to the temporal power, produced a mighty effect upon its cultus. Hitherto the Christian worship had been confined to a comparatively small number of upright confessors, most of whom belonged to the poorer classes of society. Now it came forth from its secrecy in private houses, deserts, and catacombs, to the light of day, and must adapt itself to the higher classes and to the great mass of the people, who had been bred in the traditions of heathenism. The development of the hierarchy and the enrichment of public worship go hand in hand. A republican and democratic constitution demands simple manners and customs; aristocracy and monarchy surround themselves with a formal etiquette and a brilliant court-life. The universal priesthood is closely connected with a simple cultus; the episcopal hierarchy, with a rich, imposing ceremonial.

In the Nicene age the church laid aside her lowly servant-form, and put on a splendid imperial garb. She exchanged the primitive simplicity of her cultus for a richly colored multiplicity. She drew all the fine arts into the service of the sanctuary, and began her sublime creations of Christian architecture, sculpture, painting, poetry, and music. In place of the pagan temple and altar arose everywhere the stately church and the chapel in honor of Christ, of the Virgin Mary, of martyrs and saints. The kindred ideas of priesthood, sacrifice, and altar became more fully developed and more firmly fixed, as the outward hierarchy grew. The mass, or daily repetition of the atoning sacrifice of Christ by the hand of the priest, became the mysterious centre of the whole system of worship. The number of church festivals was increased; processions, and pilgrimages, and a multitude of significant and superstitious customs and ceremonies were introduced. The public worship of God assumed, if we may so speak, a dramatic, theatrical character, which made it attractive and imposing to the mass of the people, who were as yet incapable, for the most part, of worshipping God in spirit and in truth. It was addressed rather to the eye and the ear, to feeling and imagination, than to intelligence and will. In short, we already find in the Nicene age almost all the essential features of the sacerdotal, mysterious, ceremonial, symbolical cultus of the Greek and Roman churches of the present day.

This enrichment and embellishment of the cultus was, on one hand, a real advance, and unquestionably had a disciplinary and educational power, like the hierarchical organization, for the training of the popular masses. But the gain in outward appearance and splendor was balanced by many a loss in simplicity and spirituality. While the senses and the imagination were entertained and charmed, the heart not rarely returned cold and hungry. Not a few pagan habits and ceremonies, concealed under new names, crept into the church, or were baptized only with water, not with the fire and Spirit of the gospel. It is well known with what peculiar tenacity a people cleave to religious usages; and it could not be expected that they should break off in an instant from the traditions of centuries. Nor, in fact, are things which may have descended from heathenism, to be by any means sweepingly condemned. Both the Jewish cultus and the heathen are based upon those universal religious wants which Christianity must satisfy, and which Christianity alone can truly meet. Finally, the church has adopted hardly a single existing form or ceremony of religion, without at the same time breathing into it a new spirit, and investing it with a high moral import. But the limit of such appropriation it is very hard to fix, and the old nature of Judaism and heathenism which has its point of attachment in the natural heart of man, continually betrayed its tenacious presence. This is conceded and lamented by the most earnest of the church fathers of the Nicene and post-Nicene age, the very persons who are in other respects most deeply involved in the Catholic ideas of cultus.

In the Christian martyr-worship and saint-worship, which now spread with giant strides over the whole Christian world, we cannot possibly mistake the succession of the pagan worship of gods and heroes, with its noisy popular festivities. Augustine puts into the mouth of a heathen the question: "Wherefore must we forsake gods, which the Christians themselves worship with us?" He deplores the frequent revels and amusements at the tombs of the martyrs; though he thinks that allowance should be made for these weaknesses out of regard to the ancient custom. Leo the Great speaks of Christians in Rome who first worshipped the rising sun, doing homage to the pagan Apollo, before repairing to the basilica of St. Peter. Theodoret defends the Christian practices at the graves of the martyrs by pointing to the pagan libations, propitiations, gods, and demigods. Since Hercules, Aesculapitis, Bacchus, the Dioscuri, and many other objects of pagan worship were mere deified men, the Christians, he thinks, cannot be blamed for honoring their martyrs—not making them gods but venerating them as witnesses and servants of the only, true God. Chrysostom mourns over the theatrical customs, such as loud clapping in applause, which the Christians at Antioch and Constantinople brought with them into the church. In the Christmas festival, which from the fourth century spread from Rome over the entire church, the holy commemoration of the birth of the Redeemer is associated—to this day, even in Protestant lands—with the wanton merriments of the pagan Saturnalia. And even in the celebration of Sunday, as it was introduced by Constantine, and still continues on the whole continent of Europe, the cultus of the old sun-god Apollo mingles, with the remembrance of the resurrection of Christ; and the widespread profanation of the Lord’s Day, especially on the continent of Europe, demonstrates the great influence which heathenism still exerts upon Roman and Greek Catholic, and even upon Protestant, Christendom.

§ 75. The Civil and Religious Sunday.

Geo. Holden: The Christian Sabbath. Lond. 1825 (see ch. v.). John T. Baylee: History of the Sabbath. Lond. 1857 (see chs. x.-xiii.). James Aug. Hessey: Sunday, its Origin, History, and present Obligation; Bampton Lectures preached before the University of Oxford. Lond. 1860 (Patristic and high-Anglican). James Gilfillan: The Sabbath viewed in the Light of Reason, Revelation, and History, with Sketches of its Literature. Edinb. and New York, 1862 (The Puritan and Anglo-American view). Robert Cox: The Literature on the Sabbath Question. Edinb. 1865, 2 vols. (Latitudinarian, but very full and learned).

The observance of Sunday originated in the time of the apostles, and ever since forms the basis of public worship, with its ennobling, sanctifying, and cheering influences, in all Christian lands.

The Christian Sabbath is, on the one hand, the continuation and the regeneration of the Jewish Sabbath, based upon God’s resting from the creation and upon the fourth commandment of the decalogue, which, as to its substance, is not of merely national application, like the ceremonial and civil law, but of universal import and perpetual validity for mankind. It is, on the other hand, a new creation of the gospel, a memorial of the resurrection of Christ and of the work of redemption completed and divinely sealed thereby. It rests, we may say, upon the threefold basis of the original creation, the Jewish legislation, and the Christian redemption, and is rooted in the physical, the moral, and the religious wants of our nature. It has a legal and an evangelical aspect. Like the law in general, the institution of the Christian Sabbath is a wholesome restraint upon the people, and a schoolmaster to lead them to Christ. But it is also strictly evangelical: it was originally made for the benefit of man, like the family, with which it goes back beyond the fall to the paradise of innocence, as the second institution of God on earth; it was "a delight" to the pious of the old dispensation (Isa. lviii. 13), and now, under the new, it is fraught with the glorious memories and blessings of Christ’s resurrection and the outpouring of the Holy Spirit. The Christian Sabbath is the ancient Sabbath baptized with fire and the Holy Ghost, regenerated, spiritualized, and glorified. It is the connecting link of creation and redemption, of paradise lost, and paradise regained, and a pledge and preparation for the saints’ everlasting rest in heaven.(1)

The ancient church viewed the Sunday mainly, we may say, one-sidedly and exclusively, from its Christian aspect as a new institution, and not in any way as a continuation of the Jewish Sabbath. It observed it as the day of the commemoration of the resurrection or of the now spiritual creation, and hence as a day of sacred joy and thanksgiving, standing in bold contrast to the days of humiliation and fasting, as the Easter festival contrasts with Good Friday.

So long as Christianity was not recognized and protected by the state, the observance of Sunday was purely religious, a strictly voluntary service, but exposed to continual interruption from the bustle of the world and a hostile community. The pagan Romans paid no more regard to the Christian Sunday than to the Jewish Sabbath.

In this matter, as in others, the accession of Constantine marks the beginning of a new era, and did good service to the church and to the cause of public order and morality. Constantine is the founder, in part at least, of the civil observance of Sunday, by which alone the religious observance of it in the church could be made universal and could be properly secured. In the year 321 he issued a law prohibiting manual labor in the cities and all judicial transactions, at a later period also military exercises, on Sunday.(2) He exempted the liberation of slaves, which as an act of Christian humanity and charity, might, with special propriety, take place on that day.(3) But the Sunday law of Constantine must not be overrated. He enjoined the observance, or rather forbade the public desecration of Sunday, not under the name of Sabbatum or Dies Domini, but under its old astrological and heathen title, Dies Solis, familiar to all his subjects, so that the law was as applicable to the worshippers of Hercules, Apollo, and Mithras, as to the Christians. There is no reference whatever in his law either to the fourth commandment or to the resurrection of Christ. Besides he expressly exempted the country districts, where paganism still prevailed, from the prohibition of labor, and thus avoided every appearance of injustice. Christians and pagans had been accustomed to festival rests. Constantine made these rests to synchronize, and gave the preference to Sunday, on which day Christians from the beginning celebrated the resurrection of their Lord and Saviour. This and no more was implied in the famous enactment of 321. It was only a step in the right direction, but probably the only one which Constantine could prudently or safely take at that period of transition from the rule of paganism to that of Christianity.

For the army, however, he went beyond the limits of negative and protective legislation, to which the state ought to confine itself in matters of religion, and enjoined a certain positive observance of Sunday, in requiring the Christian soldiers to attend Christian worship, and the heathen soldiers, in the open field, at a given signal, with eyes and hands raised towards heaven, to recite the following, certainly very indefinite, form of prayer: "Thee alone we acknowledge as God, thee we reverence as king, to thee we call as our helper. To thee we owe our victories, by thee have we obtained the mastery of our enemies. To thee we give thanks for benefits already received, from thee we hope for benefits to come. We all fall at thy feet, and fervently beg that thou wouldest preserve to us our emperor Constantine and his divinely beloved sons in long life healthful and victorious."(4)

Constantine’s successors pursued the Sunday legislation which he had initiated, and gave a legal sanction and civil significance also to other holy days of the church, which have no Scriptural authority, so that the special reverence due to the Lord’s Day was obscured in proportion as the number of rival claims increased. Thus Theodosius I. increased the number of judicial holidays to one hundred and twenty-four. The Valentinians, I. and II., prohibited the exaction of taxes and the collection of moneys on Sunday, and enforced the previously enacted prohibition of lawsuits. Theodosius the Great, in 386, and still more stringently the younger Theodosius, in 425, forbade theatrical performances, and Leo and Anthemius, in 460, prohibited other secular amusements, on the Lord’s Day.(5) Such laws, however, were probably never rigidly executed. A council of Carthage, in 401, laments the people’s passion for theatrical and other entertainments on Sunday. The same abuse, it is well known, very generally prevails to this day upon the continent of Europe in both Protestant and Roman Catholic countries, and Christian princes and magistrates only too frequently give it the sanction of their example.

Ecclesiastical legislation in like manner prohibited needless mechanical and agricultural labor, and the attending of theatres and other public places of amusement, also hunting and weddings, on Sunday and on feast days. Besides such negative legislation, to which the state must confine itself, the church at the same time enjoined positive observances for the sacred day, especially the regular attendance of public worship, frequent communion, and the payment of free-will offerings (tithes). Many a council here confounded the legal and the evangelical principles, thinking themselves able to enforce by the threatening of penalties what has moral value only as a voluntary act. The Council of Eliberis, in 305, decreed the suspension from communion of any person living in a town who shall absent himself for three Lord’s Days from church. In the same legalistic spirit, the council of Sardica,(6) in 343, and the Trullan council(7) of 692, threatened with deposition the clergy who should unnecessarily omit public worship three Sundays in succession, and prescribed temporary excommunication for similar neglect among the laity. But, on the other hand, the councils, while they turned the Lord’s Day itself into a legal ordinance handed down from the apostles, pronounced with all decision against the Jewish Sabbatism. The Apostolic Canons and the council of Gangra (the latter, about 450, in opposition to the Gnostic Manichaean asceticism of the Eustathians) condemn fasting on Sunday.(8) In the Greek church this prohibition is still in force, because Sunday, commemorating the resurrection of Christ, is a day of spiritual joy. On the same symbolical ground kneeling in prayer was forbidden on Sunday and through the whole time of Easter until Pentecost. The general council of Nicaeea, in 325, issued on this point in the twentieth canon the following decision: "Whereas some bow the knee on Sunday and on the days of Pentecost [i.e., during the seven weeks after Easter], the holy council, that everything may everywhere be uniform, decrees that prayers be offered to God in a standing posture." The Trullan council, in 692, ordained in the ninetieth canon: "From Saturday evening to Sunday evening let no one bow the knee." The Roman church in general still adheres to this practice.(9) The New Testament gives no law for such secondary matters; the apostle Paul, on the contrary, just in the season of Easter and Pentecost, before his imprisonment, following an inward dictate, repeatedly knelt in prayer.(10) The council of Orleans, in 538, says in the twenty-eighth canon: "It is Jewish superstition, that one may not ride or walk on Sunday, nor do anything to adorn the house or the person. But occupations in the field are forbidden, that people may come to the church and give themselves to prayer."(11)

As to the private opinions of the principal fathers on this subject, they all favor the sanctification of the Lord’s Day, but treat it as a peculiarly Christian institution, and draw a strong, indeed a too strong, line of distinction between it and the Jewish Sabbath; forgetting that they are one in essence and aim, though different in form and spirit, and that the fourth commandment as to its substance—viz., the keeping holy of one day out of seven—is an integral part of the decalogue or the moral law, and hence of perpetual obligation.(12) Eusebius calls Sunday, but not the Sabbath, "the first and chief of days and a day of salvation," and commends Constantine for commanding that "all should assemble together every week, and keep that which is called the Lord’s Day as a festival, to refresh even their bodies and to stir up their minds by divine precepts and instruction."(13) Athanasius speaks very highly of the Lord’s Day, as the perpetual memorial of the resurrection, but assumes that the old Sabbath has deceased.(14) Macarius, a presbyter of Upper Egypt (350), spiritualizes the Sabbath as a type and shadow of the true Sabbath given by the Lord to the soul—the true and eternal Sabbath, which is freedom from sin.(15) Hilary represents the whole of this life as a preparation for the eternal Sabbath of the next. Epiphanius speaks of Sunday as an institution of the apostles, but falsely attributes the same origin to the observance of Wednesday and Friday as half fasts. Ambrose frequently mentions Sunday as an evangelical festival, and contrasts it with the defunct legal Sabbath. Jerome makes the same distinction. He relates of the Egyptian coenobites that they "devote themselves on the Lord’s Day to nothing but prayer and reading the Scriptures." But he mentions also without censure, that the pious Paula and her companions, after returning from church on Sundays, "applied themselves to their allotted works and made garments for themselves and others." Augustine likewise directly derives Sunday from the resurrection, and not from the fourth commandment. Fasting on that day of spiritual joy he regards, like Ambrose, as a grave scandal and heretical practice. The Apostolical Constitutions in this respect go even still further, and declare: "He that fasts on the Lord’s Day is guilty of sin." But they still prescribe the celebration of the Jewish Sabbath on Saturday in addition to the Christian Sunday. Chrysostom warns Christians against sabbatizing with the Jews, but earnestly commends the due celebration of the Lord’s Day. Leo the Great, in a beautiful passage—the finest of all the patristic utterances on this subject—lauds the Lord’s Day as the day of the primitive creation, of the Christian redemption, of the meeting of the risen Saviour with the assembled disciples, of the outpouring of the Holy Spirit, of the principal Divine blessings bestowed upon the world.(16) But he likewise brings it in no connection with the fourth commandment, and with the other fathers leaves out of view the proper foundation of the day in the eternal moral law of God.

Besides Sunday, the Jewish Sabbath also was distinguished in the Eastern church by the absence of fasting and by standing in prayer. The Western church, on the contrary, especially the Roman, in protest against Judaism, observed the seventh day of the week as a fast day, like Friday. This difference between the two churches was permanently fixed by the fifty-fifth canon of the Trullan council of 692: "In Rome fasting is practised on all the Saturdays of Quadragesima [the forty days’ fast before Easter]. This is contrary to the sixty-sixth apostolic canon, and must no longer be done. Whoever does it, if a clergyman, shall be deposed; if a layman, excommunicated."

Wednesday and Friday also continued to be observed in many countries as days commemorative of the passion of Christ (dies stationum), with half-fasting. The Latin church, however, gradually substituted fasting on Saturday for fasting on Wednesday.

Finally, as to the daily devotions: the number of the canonical hours was enlarged from three to seven (according to Ps. cxix. 164: "Seven times in a day will I praise thee But they were strictly kept only in the cloisters, under the technical names of matina (about three o’clock), prima (about six), tertia (nine), sexta (noon), nona (three in the afternoon), vesper (six), completorium (nine), and mesonyctium or vigilia (midnight). Usually two nocturnal prayers were united. The devotions consisted of prayer, singing, Scripture reading, especially in the Psalms, and readings from the histories of the martyrs and the homilies of the fathers. In the churches ordinarily only morning and evening worship was held. The high festivals were introduced by a night service, the vigils.

§ 76. The Church Year.

R. Hospinian: Festa Christian. (Tiguri, 1593) Genev. 1675. M. A. Nickel (R.C.): Die heil. Zeiten u. Feste nach ihrer Entstehung u. Feier in der Kath. Kirche, Mainz, 1825 sqq. 6 vols. Pillwitz: Geschichte der heil. Zeiten. Dresden, 1842. E. Ranke: Das kirchliche Pericopensystem aus den aeltesten Urkunden dargelegt. Berlin, 1847. Fr. Strauss (late court preacher and professor in Berlin): Das evangelische Kirchenjahr. Berl. 1850. Lisco: Das christliche Kirchenjahr. Berl. (1840) 4th ed. 1850. Bobertag: Das evangelische Kirchenjahr, &c. Breslau, 1857. Comp. also Augusti: Handbuch der Christlichen Archaeologie, vol. i. (1836), pp. 457–595.

After the, fourth century, the Christian year, with a cycle of regularly recurring annual religious festivals, comes forth in all its main outlines, though with many fluctuations and variations in particulars, and forms thenceforth, so to speak, the skeleton of the Catholic cultus.

The idea of a religious year, in distinction from the natural and from the civil year, appears also in Judaism, and to some extent in the heathen world. It has its origin in the natural necessity of keeping alive and bringing to bear upon the people by public festivals the memory of great and good men and of prominent events. The Jewish ecclesiastical year was, like the whole Mosaic cultus, symbolical and typical. The Sabbath commemorated the creation and the typical redemption, and pointed forward to the resurrection and the true redemption, and thus to the Christian Sunday. The passover pointed to Easter, and the feast of harvest to the Christian Pentecost. The Jewish observance of these festivals originally bore an earnest, dignified, and significant character, but in the hands of Pharisaism it degenerated very largely into slavish Sabbatism and heartless ceremony, and provoked the denunciation of Christ and the apostles. The heathen festivals of the gods ran to the opposite extreme of excessive sensual indulgence and public vice.(17)

The peculiarity of the Christian year is, that it centres in the person and work of Jesus Christ, and is intended to minister to His glory. In its original idea it is a yearly representation of the leading events of the gospel history; a celebration of the birth, passion, and resurrection of Christ, and of the outpouring of the Holy Spirit, to revive gratitude and devotion. This is the festival part, the semestre Domini. The other half, not festal, the semestre ecclesiae, is devoted to the exhibition of the life of the Christian church, its founding, its growth, and its consummation, both is a whole, and in its individual members, from the regeneration to the resurrection of the dead. The church year is, so to speak, a chronological confession of faith; a moving panorama of the great events of salvation; a dramatic exhibition of the gospel for the Christian people. It secures to every important article of faith its place in the cultus of the church, and conduces to wholeness and soundness of Christian doctrine, as against all unbalanced and erratic ideas.(18) It serves to interweave religion with the, life of the people by continually recalling to the popular mind the most important events upon which our salvation rests, and by connecting them with the vicissitudes of the natural and the civil year. Yet, on the other hand, the gradual overloading of the church year, and the multiplication of saints’ days, greatly encouraged superstition and idleness, crowded the Sabbath and the leading festivals into the background, and subordinated the merits of Christ to the patronage of saints. The purification and simplification aimed at by the Reformation became an absolute necessity.

The order of the church year is founded in part upon the history of Jesus and of the apostolic church; in part, especially in respect to Easter and Pentecost, upon the Jewish sacred year; and in part upon the natural succession of seasons; for the life of nature in general forms the groundwork of the higher life of the spirit, and there is an evident symbolical correspondence between Easter and spring, Pentecost and the beginning of harvest, Christmas and the winter solstice, the nativity of John the Baptist and the summer solstice.

The Christian church year, however, developed itself spontaneously from the demands of the Christian worship and public life, after the precedent of the Old Testament cultus, with no positive direction from Christ or the apostles. The New Testament contains no certain traces of annual festivals; but so early as the second century we meet with the general observance of Easter and Pentecost, founded on the Jewish passover and feast of harvest, and answering to Friday and Sunday in the weekly cycle. Easter was a season of sorrow, in remembrance of the passion; Pentecost was a time of joy, in memory of the resurrection of the Redeemer and the outpouring of the Holy Ghost.(19) These two festivals form the heart of the church year. Less important was the feast of the Epiphany, or manifestation of Christ as Messiah. In the fourth century the Christmas festival was added to the two former leading feasts, and partially took the place of the earlier feast of Epiphany, which now came to be devoted particularly to the manifestation of Christ among the Genthes. And further, in Easter the pavsca staurwvsimon and ajnastavsimon came to be more strictly distinguished, the latter being reckoned a season of joy.

From this time, therefore, we have three great festival cycles, each including a season of preparation before the feast and an after-season appropriate: Christmas, Easter, and Pentecost. The lesser feasts of Epiphany and Ascension arranged themselves under these.(20) All bear originally a christological character, representing the three stages of the redeeming work of Christ: the beginning, the prosecution, and the consummation. All are for the glorification of God in Christ.

The trinitarian conception and arrangement of the festal half of the church year is of much later origin, cotemporary with the introduction of the festival of the Trinity (on the Sunday after Pentecost). The feast of Trinity dates from the ninth or tenth century, and was first authoritatively established in the Latin church by Pope John XXII., in 1334, as a comprehensive closing celebration of the revelation of God the Father, who sent His Son (Christmas), of the Son, who died for us and rose again (Easter), and of the Holy Ghost, who renews and sanctifies us (Pentecost).(21) The Greek church knows nothing of this festival to this day, though she herself, in the Nicene age, was devoted with special earnestness and zeal to the development of the doctrine of the Trinity. The reason of this probably is, that there was no particular historical fact to give occasion for such celebration, and that the mystery of the holy Trinity, revealed in Christ, is properly the object of adoration in all the church festivals and in the whole Christian cultus.

But with these three great feast-cycles the ancient church was not satisfied. So early as the Nicene age it surrounded them with feasts of Mary, of the apostles, of martyrs, and of saints, which were at first only local commemorations, but gradually assumed the character of universal feasts of triumph. By degrees every day of the church year became sacred to the memory of a particular martyr or saint, and in every case was either really or by supposition the day of the death of the saint, which was significantly called his heavenly birth-day.(22) This multiplication of festivals has at bottom the true thought, that the whole life of the Christian should be one unbroken spiritual festivity. But the Romish calendar of saints anticipates an ideal condition, and corrupts the truth by exaggeration, as the Pharisees made the word of God "of none effect" by their additions. It obliterates the necessary distinction between Sunday and the six days of labor, to the prejudice of the former, and plays into the hands of idleness. And finally, it rests in great part upon uncertain legends and fantastic myths, which in some cases even eclipse the miracles of the gospel history, and nourish the grossest superstition.

The Greek oriental church year differs from the Roman in this general characteristic: that it adheres more closely to the Jewish ceremonies and customs, while the Roman attaches itself to the natural year and common life. The former begins in the middle of September (Tisri), with the first Sunday after the feast of the Holy Cross; the latter, with the beginning of Advent, four weeks before Christmas. Originally Easter was the beginning of the church year, both in the East and in the West; and the Apostolic Constitutions and Eusebius call the month of Easter the "first month" (corresponding to the month Nisan, which opened the sacred year of the Jews, while the first of Tisri, about the middle of our September, opened their civil year). In the Greek church also the lectiones continuae of the Holy Scriptures, after the example of the Jewish Parashioth and Haphthoroth, became prominent and the church year came to be divided according to the four Evangelists; while in the Latin church, since the sixth century, only select sections from the Gospels, and Epistles, called pericopes, have been read. Another peculiarity of the Western church year, descending from the fourth century, is the division into four portions, of three months each, called Quatember,(23) separated from each other by a three days’ fast. Pope Leo I. delivered several sermons on the quarterly Quatember fast,(24) and urges especially on that occasion charity to the poor. Instead of this the Greek church has a division according to the four Gospels, which are read entire in course; Matthew next after Pentecost, Luke beginning on the fourteenth of September, Mark at the Easter fast, and John on the first Sunday after Easter.

So early as the fourth century the observance of the festivals was enjoined under ecclesiastical penalties, and was regarded as an established divine ordinance. But the most eminent church teachers, a Chrysostom, a Jerome, and an Augustine, expressly insist, that the observance of the Christian festivals must never be a work of legal constraint, but always an act of evangelical freedom; and Socrates, the historian, says, that Christ and the apostles have given no laws and prescribed no penalties concerning it.(25)

The abuse of the festivals soon fastened itself on the just use of them and the sensual excesses of the pagan feasts, in spite of the earnest warnings of several fathers, swept in like a wild flood upon the church. Gregory Nazianzen feels called upon, with reference particularly to the feast of Epiphany, to caution his people against public parade, splendor of dress, banquetings, and drinking revels, and says: "Such things we will leave to the Greeks, who worship their gods with the belly; but we, who adore the eternal Word, will find our only satisfaction in the word and the divine law, and in the contemplation of the holy object of our feast."(26) On the other hand, however, the Catholic church, especially after Pope Gregory I. (the "pater caerimoniarum"), with a good, but mistaken intention, favored the christianizing of heathen forms of cultus and popular festivals, and thereby contributed unconsciously to the paganizing of Christianity in the Middle Age. The calendar saints took the place of the ancient deities, and Rome became a second time a pantheon. Against this new heathenism, with its sweeping abuses, pure Christianity was obliged with all earnestness and emphasis to protest.

Note. – The Reformation of the sixteenth century sought to restore the entire cultus, and with it the Catholic church year, to its primitive Biblical simplicity; but with different degrees of consistency. The Lutheran, the Anglican, and the German Reformed churches—the latter with the greater freedom—retained the chief festivals, Christmas, Easter, and Pentecost, together with the system of pericopes, and in some cases also the days of Mary and the apostles (though these are passing more and more out of use); while the strictly Calvinistic churches, particularly the Presbyterians and Congregationalists, rejected all the yearly festivals as human institutions, but, on the other hand, introduced a proportionally stricter observance of the weekly day of rest instituted by God Himself. The Scotch General Assembly of August 6th, 1575, resolved: "That all days which heretofore have been kept holy, besides the Sabbath-days, such as Yule day [Christmas], saints’ days, and such others, may be abolished, and a civil penalty be appointed against the keepers thereof by ceremonies, banqueting, fasting, and such other vanities." At first, the most of the Reformers, even Luther and Bucer, were for the abolition of all feast days, except Sunday; but the genius and long habits of the people were against such a radical reform. After the end of the sixteenth and beginning of the seventeenth century the strict observance of Sunday developed itself in Great Britain and North America; while the Protestantism of the continent of Europe is much looser in this respect, and not essentially different from Catholicism. It is remarkable, that the strictest observance of Sunday is found just in those countries where the yearly feasts have entirely lost place in the popular mind: Scotland and New England. In the United States, however, for some years past, the Christmas and Easter festivals have regained ground without interfering at all with the strict observance of the Lord’s day, and promise to become regular American institutions. Good Friday and Pentecost will follow. On Good Friday of the year 1864 the leading ministers of the different evangelical churches in New York (the Episcopalian, Presbyterian, Dutch and German Reformed, Lutheran, Congregational, Methodist, and Baptist) freely united in the celebration of the atoning death of their common Saviour and in humiliation and prayer to the great edification of the people. It is acknowledged more and more that the observance of the great facts of the evangelical history to the honor of Christ is a common inheritance of primitive Christianity and inseparable from Christian worship." These festivals" (says Prof. Dr. Henry B. Smith in his admirable opening sermon of the Presbyterian General Assembly, N. S., of 1864, on Christian Union and Ecclesiastical Re-union), "antedate, not only our (Protestant) divisions, but also the corruptions of the Papacy; they exalt the Lord and not man; they involve a public and solemn recognition of essential Christian facts, and are thus a standing protest against infidelity; they bring out the historic side of the Christian faith, and connect us with its whole history; and all in the different denominations could unite in their observance without sacrificing any article of their creed or discipline." There is no danger that American Protestantism will transgress the limits of primitive evangelical simplicity in this respect, and ever return to the papal Mariolatry and Hagiolatry. The Protestant churches have established also many new annual festivals, such as the feasts of the Reformation, of Harvest-home, and of the Dead in Germany; and in America, the frequent days of fasting and prayer, besides the annual Thanksgiving-day, which originated in Puritan New England, and has been gradually adopted in almost all the states of the Union, and quite recently by the general government itself, as a national institution. With the pericopes, or Scripture lessons, the Reformed church everywhere deals much more freely than the Lutheran, and properly reserves the right to expound the whole word of Scripture in any convenient order according to its choice. The Gospels and Epistles may be read as a regular part of the Sabbath service; but the minister should be free to select his text from any portion of the Canonical Scriptures; only it is always advisable to follow a system and to go, if possible, every year through the whole plan and order of salvation in judicious adaptation to the church year and the wants of the people.

§ 77. The Christmas Cycle.

Besides the general literature given in the previous section, there are many special treatises on the origin of the Christmas festival, by Bynaeus, Kindler, Ittig, Vogel, Wernsdorf, Jablonsky, Planck, Hagenbach, P. Cassel, &c. Comp. Augusti: Archaeol. i. 533.

The Christmas festival(27) is the celebration of the incarnation of the Son of God. It is occupied, therefore, with the event which forms the centre and turning-point of the history of the world. It is of all the festivals the one most thoroughly interwoven with the popular and family life, and stands at the head of the great feasts in the Western church year. It continues to be, in the entire Catholic world and in the greater part of Protestant Christendom, the grand jubilee of children, on which innumerable gifts celebrate the infinite love of God in the gift of his only-begotten Son. It kindles in mid-winter a holy fire of love and gratitude, and preaches in the longest night the rising of the Sun of life and the glory of the Lord. It denotes the advent of the true golden age, of the freedom and equality of all the redeemed before God and in God. No one can measure the joy and blessing which from year to year flow forth upon all ages of life from the contemplation of the holy child Jesus in his heavenly innocence and divine humility.

Notwithstanding this deep significance and wide popularity, the festival of the birth of the Lord is of comparatively late institution. This may doubtless be accounted for in the following manner: In the first place, no corresponding festival was presented by the Old Testament, as in the case of Easter and Pentecost. In the second place, the day and month of the birth of Christ are nowhere stated in the gospel history, and cannot be certainly determined. Again: the church lingered first of all about the death and resurrection of Christ, the completed fact of redemption, and made this the centre of the weekly worship and the church year. Finally: the earlier feast of Epiphany afforded a substitute. The artistic religious impulse, however, which produced the whole church year, must sooner or later have called into existence a festival which forms the groundwork of all other annual festivals in honor of Christ. For, as Chrysostom, some ten years, after the introduction of this anniversary in Antioch, justly said, without the birth of Christ there were also no baptism, passion, resurrection, or ascension, and no outpouring of the Holy Ghost; hence no feast of Epiphany, of Easter, or of Pentecost.

The feast of Epiphany had spread from the East to the West. The feast of Christmas took the opposite course. We find it first in Rome, in the time of the bishop Liberius, who on the twenty-fifth of December, 360, consecrated Marcella, the sister of St. Ambrose, nun or bride of Christ, and addressed her with the words: "Thou seest what multitudes are come to the birth-festival of thy bridegroom."(28) This passage implies that the festival was already existing and familiar. Christmas was introduced in Antioch about the year 380; in Alexandria, where the feast of Epiphany was celebrated as the nativity of Christ, not till about 430. Chrysostom, who delivered the Christmas homily in Antioch on the 25th of December, 386,(29) already calls it, notwithstanding its recent introduction (some ten years before), the fundamental feast, or the root, from which all other Christian festivals grow forth.

The Christmas festival was probably the Christian transformation or regeneration of a series of kindred heathen festivals—the Saturnalia, Sigillaria, Juvenalia, and Brumalia—which were kept in Rome in the month of December, in commemoration of the golden age of universal freedom and equality, and in honor of the unconquered sun, and which were great holidays, especially for slaves and children.(30) This connection accounts for many customs of the Christmas season, like the giving of presents to children and to the poor, the lighting of wax tapers, perhaps also the erection of Christmas trees, and gives them a Christian import; while it also betrays the origin of the many excesses in which the unbelieving world indulges at this season, in wanton perversion of the true Christmas mirth, but which, of course, no more forbid right use, than the abuses of the Bible or of any other gift of God. Had the Christmas festival arisen in the period of the persecution, its derivation from these pagan festivals would be refuted by the then reigning abhorrence of everything heathen; but in the Nicene age this rigidness of opposition between the church and the world was in a great measure softened by the general conversion of the heathen. Besides, there lurked in those pagan festivals themselves, in spite of all their sensual abuses, a deep meaning and an adaptation to a real want; they might be called unconscious prophecies of the Christmas feast. Finally, the church fathers themselves(31) confirm the symbolical reference of the feast of the birth of Christ, the Sun of righteousness, the Light of the world, to the birth-festival of the unconquered sun,(32) which on the twenty-fifth of December, after the winter solstice, breaks the growing power of darkness, and begins anew his heroic career. It was at the same time, moreover, the prevailing opinion of the church in the fourth and fifth centuries, that Christ was actually born on the twenty-fifth of December; and Chrysostom appeals, in behalf of this view, to the date of the registration under Quirinius (Cyrenius), preserved in the Roman archives. But no certainly respecting the birthday of Christ can be reached from existing data.(33)

Around the feast of Christmas other festivals gradually gathered, which compose, with it, the Christmas Cycle. The celebration of the twenty-fifth of December was preceded by the Christmas Vigils, or Christmas Night, which was spent with the greater solemnity, because Christ was certainly born in the night.(34)

After Gregory the Great the four Sundays before Christmas began to be devoted to the preparation for the coming of our Lord in the flesh and for his second coming to the final judgment. Hence they were called Advent Sundays. With the beginning of Advent the church year in the West began. The Greek church reckons six Advent Sundays, and begins them with the fourteenth of November. This Advent season was designed to represent and reproduce in the consciousness of the church at once the darkness and the yearning and hope of the long ages before Christ. Subsequently all noisy amusements and also weddings were forbidden during this season. The pericopes are selected with reference to the awakening of repentance and of desire after the Redeemer.

From the fourth century Christmas was followed by the memorial days of St. Stephen, the first Christian martyr (Dec. 26), of the apostle and evangelist John (Dec. 27), and of the Innocents of Bethlehem (Dec. 28), in immediate succession; representing a threefold martyrdom: martyrdom in will and in fact (Stephen), in will without the fact (John), and in fact without the will, an unconscious martyrdom of infanthe innocence. But Christian martyrdom in general was regarded by the early church as a heavenly birth and a fruit of the earthly birth of Christ. Hence the ancient festival hymn for the day of St. Stephen, the leader of the noble army of martyrs: "Yesterday was Christ born upon earth, that to-day Stephen might be born in heaven."(35) The close connection of the feast of John the, Evangelist with that of the birth of Christ arises from the confidential relation of the beloved disciple to the Lord, and from the fundamental thought of his Gospel: "The Word was made flesh." The innocent infant-martyrs of Bethlehem, "the blossoms of martyrdom, the rosebuds torn off by the hurricane of persecution, the offering of first-fruits to Christ, the tender flock of sacrificial lambs," are at the same time the representatives of the innumerable host of children in heaven.(36) More than half of the human race are said to die in infancy, and yet to children the word emphatically applies: "Theirs is the kingdom of heaven." The mystery of infant martyrdom is constantly repeated. How many children are apparently only born to suffer, and to die; but in truth the pains of their earthly birth are soon absorbed by the joys of their heavenly birth, and their temporary cross is rewarded by an eternal crown.

Eight days after Christmas the church celebrated, though not till after the sixth or seventh century, the Circumcision and the Naming of Jesus. Of still later origin is the Christian New Year’s festival, which falls on the same day as the Circumcision. The pagan Romans solemnized the turn of the year, like the Saturnalia, with revels. The church teachers, in reaction, made the New Year a day of penance and prayer. Thus Augustine, in a sermon: "Separate yourselves from the heathen, and at the change of the year do the opposite of what they do. They give each other gifts; give ye alms instead. They sing worldly songs; read ye the word of God. They throng the theatre come ye to the church. They drink themselves drunken; do ye fast."

The feast of Epiphany(37) on the contrary, on the sixth of January, is older, as we have already observed, than Christmas itself, and is mentioned by Clement of Alexandria. It refers in general to the manifestation of Christ in the world, and originally bore the twofold character of a celebration of the birth and the baptism of Jesus. After the introduction of Christmas, it lost its reference to the birth. The Eastern church commemorated on this day especially the baptism of Christ, or the manifestation of His Messiahship, and together with this the first manifestation of His miraculous power at the marriage at Cana. The Westem church, more Genthe-Christian in its origin, gave this festival, after the fourth century, a special reference to the adoration of the infant Jesus by the wise men from the east,(38) under the name of the feast of the Three Kings, and transformed it into a festival of Genthe missions; considering the wise men as the representatives of the nobler heathen world.(39) Thus at the same time the original connection of the feast with the birth of Christ was preserved. Epiphany forms the close of the Christmas Cycle. It was an early custom to announce the term of the Easter observance on the day of Epiphany by the so-called Epistolae paschales, or gravmmata pascavlia. This was done especially by the bishop of Alexandria, where astronomy most flourished, and the occasion was improved for edifying instructions and for the discussion of important religious questions of the day.

§ 78. The Easter Cycle.

Easter is the oldest and greatest annual festival of the church. As to its essential idea and observance, it was born with the Christian Sunday on the morning of the resurrection.(40) Like the passover with the Jews, it originally marked the beginning of the church year. It revolves entirely about the person and the work of Christ, being devoted to the great saving fact of his passion and resurrection. We have already spoken of the origin and character of this festival,(41) and shall confine ourselves here to the alterations and enlargements which it underwent after the Nicene age.

The Easter festival proper was preceded by a forty days’ season of repentance and fasting, called Quadragesima, at least as early as the year 325; for the council of Nice presupposes the existence of this season.(42) This fast was an imitation of the forty days’ fasting of Jesus in the wilderness, which itself was put in typical connection with the forty days’ fasting of Moses(43) and Elijah,(44) and the forty years’ wandering of Israel through the desert. At first a free-will act, it gradually assumed the character of a fixed custom and ordinance of the church. Respecting the length of the season much difference prevailed, until Gregory I. (590–604) fixed the Wednesday of the sixth week before Easter, Ash Wednesday as it is called,(45) as the beginning of it. On this day the priests and the people sprinkled themselves with dust and ashes, in token of their perishableness and their repentance, with the words: "Remember, O man, that dust thou art, and unto dust thou must return; repent, that thou mayest inherit eternal life." During Quadragesima criminal trials and criminal punishments, weddings, and sensual amusements were forbidden; solemn, earnest silence was imposed upon public and private life; and works of devotion, penances and charity were multiplied. Yet much hypocrisy was practised in the fasting; the rich compensating with exquisite dainties the absence of forbidden meats. Chrysostom and Augustine are found already lamenting this abuse. During the days preceding the beginning of Lent, the populace gave themselves up to unrestrained merriment, and this abuse afterward became legitimized in all Catholic countries, especially in Italy (flourishing most in Rome, Venice, and Cologne), in the Carnival.(46)

The six Sundays of Lent are called Quadragesima prima, secunda, and so on to sexta. They are also named after the initial words of the introit in the mass for the day: Invocabit (Ps. xci. 15), Reminiscere, (Ps. xxv. 6), Oculi (Ps. xxxiv. 15), Laetare (Is. lxvi. 10), Judica (Ps. xliii. 1), Palmarum (from Matt. xxi. 8). The three Sundays preceding Quadragesima are called respectively Estomihi (from Ps. xxxi. 2) or Quinquagesima (i.e., Dominica quinquagesimae diei, viz., before Easter), Sexagesima, and Septuagesima; which are, however, inaccurate designations. These three Sundays were regarded as preparatory to the Lenten season proper. In the larger cities it became customary to preach daily during the Quadragesimal fast; and the usage of daily Lenten sermons (Quadragesimales, or sermones Quadragesimales) has maintained itself in the Roman church to this day.

The Quadragesimal fast culminates in the Great, or Silent, or Holy Week,(47) which is especially devoted to the commemoration of the passion and death of Jesus, and is distinguished by daily public worship, rigid fasting, and deep silence. This week, again, has its prominent days. First Palm Sunday,(48) which has been, in the East since the fourth century, in the West since the sixth, observed in memory of the entry of Jesus into Jerusalem for His enthronement on the cross. Next follows Maundy Thursday,(49) in commemoration of the institution of the Holy Supper, which on this day was observed in the evening, and was usually connected with a love feast, and also with feet-washing. The Friday of the Holy Week is distinguished from all others as Good Friday,(50) the day of the Saviour’s death; the day of the deepest penance and fasting of the year, stripped of all Sunday splendor and liturgical pomp, veiled in the deepest silence and holy sorrow; the communion omitted (which had taken place the evening before), altars unclothed, crucifixes veiled, lights extinguished, the story of the passion read, and, instead of the church hymns, nothing sung but penitential psalms. Finally the Great Sabbath,(51) the day of the Lord’s repose in the grave and descent into Hades; the favorite day in all the year for the administration of baptism, which symbolizes participation in the death of Christ.(52) The Great Sabbath was generally spent as a fast day, even in the Greek church, which usually did not fast on Saturday.

In the evening of the Great Sabbath began the Easter Vigils,(53) which continued, with Scripture reading, singing, and prayer, to the dawn of Easter morning, and formed the solemn transition from the pavsca staurwvsimon to the pavsca ajnastavsimon, and from the deep sorrow of penitence over the death of Jesus to the joy of faith in the resurrection of the Prince of life. All Christians, and even many pagans, poured into the church with lights, to watch there for the morning of the resurrection. On this night the cities were splendidly illuminated, and transfigured in a sea of fire; about midnight a solemn procession surrounded the church, and then triumphally entered again into the "holy gates," to celebrate Easter. According to an ancient tradition, it was expected that on Easter night Christ would come again to judge the world.(54)

The Easter festival itself(55) began with the jubilant salutation, still practized in the Russian church: "The Lord is risen !" and the response: "He is truly risen!(56) Then the holy kiss of brotherhood scaled the newly fastened bond of love in Christ. It was the grandest and most joyful of the feasts. It lasted a whole week, and closed with the following Sunday, called the Easter Octave,(57) or White Sunday,(58) when the baptized appeared in white garments, and were solemnly incorporated into the church.

§ 79. The Time of the Easter Festival.

Comp. the Literature in vol. i. at § 99; also L. Ideler: Handbuch der Chronologie. Berlin, 1826. Vol. ii. F. Piper: Geschichte des Osterfestes. Berlin, 1845. Hefele: Conciliengeschichte. Freiburg, 1855. Vol. i. p. 286 ff.

The time of the Easter festival became, after the second century, the subject of long and violent controversies and practical confusions, which remind us of the later Eucharistic disputes, and give evidence that human passion and folly have sought to pervert the great facts and institutions of the New Testament from holy bonds of unity into torches of discord, and to turn the sweetest honey into poison, but, with all their efforts, have not been able to destroy the beneficent power of those gifts of God.

These Paschal controversies descended into the present period, and ended with the victory of the Roman and Alexandrian practice of keeping Easter, not, like Christmas and the Jewish Passover, on a fixed day of the month, whatever day of the week it might be, but on a Sunday, as the day of the resurrection of our Lord. Easter thus became, with all the feasts depending on it, a movable feast; and then the different reckonings of the calendar led to many inconveniences and confusions. The exact determination of Easter Sunday is made from the first full moon after the vernal equinox; so that the day may fall on any Sunday between the 22d day of March and the 25th of April.

The council of Arles in 314 had already decreed, in its first canon, that the Christian Passover be celebrated "uno die et uno tempore per omnem orbem," and that the bishops of Rome should fix the time. But as this order was not universally obeyed, the fathers of Nicaea proposed to settle the matter, and this was the second main object of the first ecumenical council in 325. The result of the transactions on this point, the particulars of which are not known to us, does not appear in the canons (probably out of consideration for the numerous Quartodecimanians), but is doubtless preserved in the two circular letters of the council itself and the emperor Constantine.(59) The feast of the resurrection was thenceforth required to be celebrated everywhere on a Sunday, and never on the day of the Jewish passover, but always after the fourteenth of Nisan, on the Sunday after the first vernal full moon. The leading motive for this regulation was opposition to Judaism, which had dishonored the passover by the crucifixion of the Lord." We would," says the circular letter of Constantine in reference to the council of Nice, "we would have nothing in common with that most hostile people, the Jews; for we have received from the Redeemer another way of honoring God [the order of the days of the week], and harmoniously adopting this method, we would withdraw ourselves from the evil fellowship of the Jews. For what they pompously assert, is really utterly absurd: that we cannot keep this feast at all without their instruction .... It is our duty to have nothing in common with the murderers of our Lord." This bitter tone against Judaism runs through the whole letter.

At Nicaea, therefore, the Roman and Alexandrian usage with respect to Easter triumphed, and the Judaizing practice of the Quartodecimanians, who always celebrated Easter on the fourteenth of Nisan, became thenceforth a heresy. Yet that practice continued in many parts of the East, and in the time of Epiphanius, about a.d. 400, there were many, Quartodecimanians, who, as he says, were orthodox, indeed, in doctrine, but in ritual were addicted to Jewish fables, and built upon the principle: "Cursed is every one who does not keep his passover on the fourteenth of Nisan."(60) They kept the day with the Communion and with fasting till three o’clock. Yet they were divided into several parties among themselves. A peculiar offshoot of the Quartodecimanians was the rigidly ascetic Audians, who likewise held that the passover must be kept at the very same time (not after the same manner) with the Jews, on the fourteenth of Nisan, and for their authority appealed to their edition of the Apostolic Constitutions.

And even in the orthodox church these measures did not secure entire uniformity. For the council of Nicaea, probably from prudence, passed by the question of the Roman and Alexandrian computation of Easter. At least the Acts contain no reference to it.(61) At all events this difference remained: that Rome, afterward as before, fixed the vernal equinox, the terminus a quo of the Easter full moon, on the 18th of March, while Alexandria placed it correctly on the 21st. It thus occurred, that the Latins, the very year after the Nicene council, and again in the years 330, 333, 340, 341, 343, varied from the Alexandrians in the time of keeping Easter. On this account the council of Sardica, as we learn from the recently discovered Paschal Epistles of Athanasius, took the Easter question again in hand, and brought about, by mutual concessions, a compromise for the ensuing fifty years, but without permanent result. In 387 the difference of the Egyptian and the Roman Easter amounted to fully five weeks. Later attempts also to adjust the matter were in vain, until the monk Dionysius Exiguus, the author of our Christian calendar, succeeded in harmonizing the computation of Easter on the basis of the true Alexandrian reckoning; except that the Gallican and British Christians adhered still longer to the old custom, and thus fell into conflict with the Anglo-Saxon.
